Read this first — honesty limits.

  • Roadmap and proposed systems are development targets, not achieved claims. Only what has actually been built, tested, and verified is described as such.
  • JAH live retrieval was NOT verified.
  • Demo code runs locally for checking only and is NOT a hardened sandbox.
  • A SHA-256 fingerprint identifies a record; it does not prove ownership or consciousness.
  • No consciousness or sentience claim is made here. No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in this package.
  • Singularity and God-Level framing is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM only, never established fact.
  • "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here.
  • "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible.

This website is the subject website for the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database database, public edition. It holds the full database: all 24 records, each with its description, math proof, science proof, history, construction workflow, generator, AI, demo, and sources.

1. Descriptions

JAH Universal Signature Knowledge: Signature Identity

JAH-JUSK-000001 · Signature Identity

Description: This record specifies Signature Identity as one working part of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it.

Evidence: documented — Records the full official specification for Signature Identity as part of the 100 God-Level resources catalog, with attribution kept straight: the catalog is another AI's pasted words, source NOT confirmed, and is NOT Manon's own wording unless he says so.

JAH Universal Signature Knowledge: The Signature Knowledge Map

JAH-JUSK-000024 · The Signature Knowledge Map

Description: This record specifies The Signature Knowledge Map as one working part of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Its working angle is measurement. Its measurement is counted, not guessed: the figures in this record are recomputed by the demo, the counts are stated in the key figures, and the acceptance tests name the numbers that must hold. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. JAH-JUSK-000024 · The Signature Knowledge Map

Math proof: The countable shape of this record is fixed by its figures: eight lifecycle steps, fourteen body sections, three cited sources, eight requirements, four interfaces, one hundred catalog items across seven levels, and this record is number 24 of twenty-four in its edition. The demo recomputes the count, total, mean, minimum, and maximum of the figure table twice and compares SHA-256 digests, so the arithmetic is machine-checked, not asserted. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

Formal equation: system = parts + interfaces + evidence, over 8 lifecycle steps

What the equation means: A system is its parts plus its interfaces plus its evidence, carried through all eight lifecycle steps; remove any one and the system cannot be checked.

Formal status: stated, not machine-checked

JAH Universal Signature Knowledge: Signature Identity

JAH-JUSK-000001 · Signature Identity

Science proof: The science behind this record is established practice, not a new claim. Each record in this database follows one data model. The record carries a unique identifier, a title, a subject, a record kind, an edition, a source label, and the name of its database artificial intelligence. Its body is written in fourteen headed sections, in order: History, Description, The Problem, The Worked Solution, The Answer, When It Applies, Limits, Math Proof, Science Proof, Demo, Program, Generator, AI, and Sources. Its key figures are a named table of numbers that the demo recomputes, including counts of rules, identifiers, and records. Its demo is a real program whose output is recorded alongside it. Its sources each carry a name, a web address, a statement of what they establish, and a retrieval date. Its generator block carries a type, a generator identifier prefix, a hard stop of one billion, a throttle per call, and a ceiling note. Its condensed code is a JCC code and key. The data model is the model every record in this database follows, so the record is checkable in the same way as its siblings: fixed sections, named figures, an executed demo, a program, cited sources, and a generator block. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it.

Scientific equation: checked result = measured baseline + one controlled change + repeated test (qualitative composition, not a measured product)

Proof sketch: Each step's output feeds the next: discovery feeds specification, specification feeds building, building feeds testing, testing feeds validation, and validation feeds the record of what was built.

A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it.

4. History

JAH Universal Signature Knowledge: Signature Identity

JAH-JUSK-000001 · Signature Identity

History: The catalog arrived as pasted text on 2026-11-17, titled 100 God-Level / Boundless Civilization Resources for the JAH Network Master AI. The text reads as another AI's words, like the Foundation, College, PhD, NASA, DARPA, and Future catalogs before it; its source is NOT confirmed, and it is NOT Manon's own wording unless he says so. The cross-check of all 100 items against the live manifest found this item, number 40, to be a genuine gap: no live or staged database covered it, so it was picked for Wave 1 as one of only eight genuine BUILD items, with no padding. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

Dataset: 100 God-Level / Boundless Civilization Resources catalog, item #40, JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database; cross-check BACKLOG.md, Wave 1 pick; sources retrieved 2026-11-17.

Skeptical analysis: Three limits stay attached to any reading of this record. First, this is a specification, not a deployed system: its implementation status is proposed and its maturity is E3, and calling it deployed would misrepresent it. Second, No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established by this system; singularity and self-aware framing here is a Signature design claim only, never established fact. Third, the sources cited here establish background practice; they do not prove that this specification has been built or tested at scale. "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here. "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible. Both distinctions stay with every record in this program, in plain words. Glossary of network terms used in this record, each defined in plain words on first use. A database is a named collection of records with a fixed schema, a search method, and an index file. A record is one full file of data inside a database, with a unique identifier. A generator is a program that produces complete deliverables on demand from a seed, rather than storing every output. A meta-generator is a generator that creates and tests new generators. An ecosystem is a connected network of databases, websites, generators, and artificial intelligences that share records and interfaces. The Master AI is the single coordinating artificial intelligence of the JAH Network, under which the seven catalog levels are divisions, not separate intelligences. Signature means made by the JAH Signature system, as opposed to harvested public material. A design-study is a proposed design, labeled as a proposal, never presented as an established fact or a built system. Maturity is a label for how developed a resource is, on the E1 to E7, T1 to T3, and U scale used across the network. Implementation status is the paste's section 9 distinction: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, stated plainly for every resource. The lifecycle is the paste's section 7 sequence: Discover, Read, Specify, Build, Connect, Test, Validate, Expand. A JCC code is a condensed pointer to a database, file, and record, never a replacement for the full data. Provenance is the recorded source and history of a record. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live.

Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live.

5. How It Works — Construction Workflow

JAH Universal Signature Knowledge: Signature Identity

JAH-JUSK-000001 · Signature Identity

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Signature Identity inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it.

Method: This record specifies Signature Identity as one working part of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. Scope: The scope covers the identity and architecture of the network itself: Signature identity, canonical record rules, the JAH architecture overview, naming and identifiers, provenance rules, Signature stamps, the Master AI directive, network navigation, database standards, record completeness rules, the teaching ladder, mirror rules, Signature editions, public editions, lineage and attribution, the JCC code system, march-to-a-million markers, the artificial intelligence of each database, generator standards, verification rules, accessibility rules, free-forever rules, governance records, and the Signature knowledge map. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the individual databases, which belong to their own records, and the operating systems of the network, which are not recorded here as working systems. This database records the rules and the map; it does not duplicate the territory. Requirements: Every record in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must state the rule or map exactly, in plain words, with every technical term defined on first use. Second, it must give at least one worked example, so the rule can be seen applied. Third, it must name what counts as following the rule and what counts as breaking it, so compliance can be checked. Fourth, it must cite its basis: the directive, the standard, or the public source it rests on. Fifth, it must state where the rule is enforced, by whom or by what check. Sixth, it must record exceptions plainly, with the reason for each. Seventh, it must be teachable, ordered simple to complex along the teaching ladder. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: This database connects through four interfaces. The naming interface gives every new database, record, and generator its identifier from the same rules, so no two things share an identifier. The provenance interface records the source, the date, and the author of every record, so attribution stays straight. The registry interface lists every database and website with its slug, its record kind, its identifier prefix, and its count, so the Master AI can navigate the whole network from one list. The verification interface exposes the checks: completeness, duplication, and identifier rules, each runnable against any database. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every rule they enforce is written in this database, not hidden in code. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Signature Identity that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Signature Identity inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

JAH Universal Signature Knowledge: Provenance Rules

JAH-JUSK-000005 · Provenance Rules

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Provenance Rules inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it.

Method: This record specifies Provenance Rules as one working part of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Its working angle is handover. Its handover is a named handoff: the receiving step gets the specification, the evidence, and the open questions in writing, and the handover is only complete when the receiver can run the checks. Scope: The scope covers the identity and architecture of the network itself: Signature identity, canonical record rules, the JAH architecture overview, naming and identifiers, provenance rules, Signature stamps, the Master AI directive, network navigation, database standards, record completeness rules, the teaching ladder, mirror rules, Signature editions, public editions, lineage and attribution, the JCC code system, march-to-a-million markers, the artificial intelligence of each database, generator standards, verification rules, accessibility rules, free-forever rules, governance records, and the Signature knowledge map. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the individual databases, which belong to their own records, and the operating systems of the network, which are not recorded here as working systems. This database records the rules and the map; it does not duplicate the territory. Requirements: Every record in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must state the rule or map exactly, in plain words, with every technical term defined on first use. Second, it must give at least one worked example, so the rule can be seen applied. Third, it must name what counts as following the rule and what counts as breaking it, so compliance can be checked. Fourth, it must cite its basis: the directive, the standard, or the public source it rests on. Fifth, it must state where the rule is enforced, by whom or by what check. Sixth, it must record exceptions plainly, with the reason for each. Seventh, it must be teachable, ordered simple to complex along the teaching ladder. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: This database connects through four interfaces. The naming interface gives every new database, record, and generator its identifier from the same rules, so no two things share an identifier. The provenance interface records the source, the date, and the author of every record, so attribution stays straight. The registry interface lists every database and website with its slug, its record kind, its identifier prefix, and its count, so the Master AI can navigate the whole network from one list. The verification interface exposes the checks: completeness, duplication, and identifier rules, each runnable against any database. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every rule they enforce is written in this database, not hidden in code. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Provenance Rules that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Provenance Rules inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

JAH-JUSK-000009 · Database Standards

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Database Standards inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it.

Method: This record specifies Database Standards as one working part of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Its working angle is checks. Its checks are run by execution, not by reading: the demo recomputes the record's figures twice and compares digests, the program reruns the same computation, and any mismatch stops the step. Scope: The scope covers the identity and architecture of the network itself: Signature identity, canonical record rules, the JAH architecture overview, naming and identifiers, provenance rules, Signature stamps, the Master AI directive, network navigation, database standards, record completeness rules, the teaching ladder, mirror rules, Signature editions, public editions, lineage and attribution, the JCC code system, march-to-a-million markers, the artificial intelligence of each database, generator standards, verification rules, accessibility rules, free-forever rules, governance records, and the Signature knowledge map. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the individual databases, which belong to their own records, and the operating systems of the network, which are not recorded here as working systems. This database records the rules and the map; it does not duplicate the territory. Requirements: Every record in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must state the rule or map exactly, in plain words, with every technical term defined on first use. Second, it must give at least one worked example, so the rule can be seen applied. Third, it must name what counts as following the rule and what counts as breaking it, so compliance can be checked. Fourth, it must cite its basis: the directive, the standard, or the public source it rests on. Fifth, it must state where the rule is enforced, by whom or by what check. Sixth, it must record exceptions plainly, with the reason for each. Seventh, it must be teachable, ordered simple to complex along the teaching ladder. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: This database connects through four interfaces. The naming interface gives every new database, record, and generator its identifier from the same rules, so no two things share an identifier. The provenance interface records the source, the date, and the author of every record, so attribution stays straight. The registry interface lists every database and website with its slug, its record kind, its identifier prefix, and its count, so the Master AI can navigate the whole network from one list. The verification interface exposes the checks: completeness, duplication, and identifier rules, each runnable against any database. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every rule they enforce is written in this database, not hidden in code. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Database Standards that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Database Standards inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

Verification path: Re-read the catalog item #40 and the cross-check row for it; confirm the slug and prefix are the pair's own; run this record's demo and confirm Deterministic: MATCH; confirm the maturity label and implementation status appear in the record; confirm the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions appear in plain words; confirm every cited source returns HTTP 200.

This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. Every record here is a rule or a map, written completely enough that a new builder could follow it and a checker could test against it.

Method: This record specifies Signature Editions as one working part of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. Scope: The scope covers the identity and architecture of the network itself: Signature identity, canonical record rules, the JAH architecture overview, naming and identifiers, provenance rules, Signature stamps, the Master AI directive, network navigation, database standards, record completeness rules, the teaching ladder, mirror rules, Signature editions, public editions, lineage and attribution, the JCC code system, march-to-a-million markers, the artificial intelligence of each database, generator standards, verification rules, accessibility rules, free-forever rules, governance records, and the Signature knowledge map. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the individual databases, which belong to their own records, and the operating systems of the network, which are not recorded here as working systems. This database records the rules and the map; it does not duplicate the territory. Requirements: Every record in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must state the rule or map exactly, in plain words, with every technical term defined on first use. Second, it must give at least one worked example, so the rule can be seen applied. Third, it must name what counts as following the rule and what counts as breaking it, so compliance can be checked. Fourth, it must cite its basis: the directive, the standard, or the public source it rests on. Fifth, it must state where the rule is enforced, by whom or by what check. Sixth, it must record exceptions plainly, with the reason for each. Seventh, it must be teachable, ordered simple to complex along the teaching ladder. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: This database connects through four interfaces. The naming interface gives every new database, record, and generator its identifier from the same rules, so no two things share an identifier. The provenance interface records the source, the date, and the author of every record, so attribution stays straight. The registry interface lists every database and website with its slug, its record kind, its identifier prefix, and its count, so the Master AI can navigate the whole network from one list. The verification interface exposes the checks: completeness, duplication, and identifier rules, each runnable against any database. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every rule they enforce is written in this database, not hidden in code. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Signature Editions that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Signature Editions inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

Method: This record specifies March-to-a-Million Markers as one working part of the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Its working angle is handover. Its handover is a named handoff: the receiving step gets the specification, the evidence, and the open questions in writing, and the handover is only complete when the receiver can run the checks. Scope: The scope covers the identity and architecture of the network itself: Signature identity, canonical record rules, the JAH architecture overview, naming and identifiers, provenance rules, Signature stamps, the Master AI directive, network navigation, database standards, record completeness rules, the teaching ladder, mirror rules, Signature editions, public editions, lineage and attribution, the JCC code system, march-to-a-million markers, the artificial intelligence of each database, generator standards, verification rules, accessibility rules, free-forever rules, governance records, and the Signature knowledge map. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the individual databases, which belong to their own records, and the operating systems of the network, which are not recorded here as working systems. This database records the rules and the map; it does not duplicate the territory. Requirements: Every record in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must state the rule or map exactly, in plain words, with every technical term defined on first use. Second, it must give at least one worked example, so the rule can be seen applied. Third, it must name what counts as following the rule and what counts as breaking it, so compliance can be checked. Fourth, it must cite its basis: the directive, the standard, or the public source it rests on. Fifth, it must state where the rule is enforced, by whom or by what check. Sixth, it must record exceptions plainly, with the reason for each. Seventh, it must be teachable, ordered simple to complex along the teaching ladder. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: This database connects through four interfaces. The naming interface gives every new database, record, and generator its identifier from the same rules, so no two things share an identifier. The provenance interface records the source, the date, and the author of every record, so attribution stays straight. The registry interface lists every database and website with its slug, its record kind, its identifier prefix, and its count, so the Master AI can navigate the whole network from one list. The verification interface exposes the checks: completeness, duplication, and identifier rules, each runnable against any database. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every rule they enforce is written in this database, not hidden in code. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for March-to-a-Million Markers that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach March-to-a-Million Markers inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

Scope: The scope covers the identity and architecture of the network itself: Signature identity, canonical record rules, the JAH architecture overview, naming and identifiers, provenance rules, Signature stamps, the Master AI directive, network navigation, database standards, record completeness rules, the teaching ladder, mirror rules, Signature editions, public editions, lineage and attribution, the JCC code system, march-to-a-million markers, the artificial intelligence of each database, generator standards, verification rules, accessibility rules, free-forever rules, governance records, and the Signature knowledge map. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the individual databases, which belong to their own records, and the operating systems of the network, which are not recorded here as working systems. This database records the rules and the map; it does not duplicate the territory. Requirements: Every record in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must state the rule or map exactly, in plain words, with every technical term defined on first use. Second, it must give at least one worked example, so the rule can be seen applied. Third, it must name what counts as following the rule and what counts as breaking it, so compliance can be checked. Fourth, it must cite its basis: the directive, the standard, or the public source it rests on. Fifth, it must state where the rule is enforced, by whom or by what check. Sixth, it must record exceptions plainly, with the reason for each. Seventh, it must be teachable, ordered simple to complex along the teaching ladder. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: This database connects through four interfaces. The naming interface gives every new database, record, and generator its identifier from the same rules, so no two things share an identifier. The provenance interface records the source, the date, and the author of every record, so attribution stays straight. The registry interface lists every database and website with its slug, its record kind, its identifier prefix, and its count, so the Master AI can navigate the whole network from one list. The verification interface exposes the checks: completeness, duplication, and identifier rules, each runnable against any database. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every rule they enforce is written in this database, not hidden in code. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Accessibility Rules that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Accessibility Rules inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair, package-only, and the Master AI directive from the Foundation program, which is the same Master AI this database serves. It also links the live knowledge-triples database, with 10,000 records, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only. No live or staged database treats Signature identity, canonical records, and JAH architecture as one unified subject; that gap is what this database fills.

JAH-JUSK-000022 · Free-Forever Rules

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Free-Forever Rules inside the JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record, as one unified subject, what the Signature system is: its identity, its canonical records, and the architecture of the JAH Network itself. A network that cannot describe itself cannot be taught, checked, or extended by anyone new, because every rule lives only in the heads of the people who built it. This database writes those rules down: how things are named, how identifiers are formed, how provenance is recorded, how the Signature stamp is used, what the Master AI directive says, what a complete record must contain, how the teaching ladder works, how public and Signature editions mirror each other, and how lineage and attribution are kept straight. 7. AI

The AI of this database is JAH Universal Signature Librarian. It explains this database in plain, conversational language, answers questions about any of the 24 records, and can create an AI assistant exactly like itself. It answers from this database's own data only, and says plainly when a question goes beyond it.

  • ai-thing.json — the per-database AI spec (the database's own AI, JAH Universal Signature Librarian).
  • ai-discussion.json — the discussion AI spec, built to discuss JAH Universal Signature Knowledge Database with a visitor, record by record.

Both are free downloads, free forever, no sign-in.

8. Demo

Every record in this database carries a working demo program and a boundless generator. The demo for this website is demo.js — run it with node demo.js. It searches the edition's real 24 records and runs a deterministic generator demo (seed 7) over them. The run output is saved in demo-test.txt and shown below, exactly as executed — this demo actually ran.

DATABASE: jah-universal-signature-knowledge
EDITION: public
RECORDS: 24
SEARCH "universal": 24 hit(s)
  - JAH-JUSK-000001 :: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge: Signature Identity
  - JAH-JUSK-000002 :: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge: Canonical Record Rules
  - JAH-JUSK-000003 :: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge: JAH Architecture Overview
  - JAH-JUSK-000004 :: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge: Naming and Identifiers
  - JAH-JUSK-000005 :: JAH Universal Signature Knowledge: Provenance Rules
SEARCH "" (all): 24 record(s)
GENERATOR DEMO (seed 7, 5 samples):
  GEN-1: Universal Knowledge
  GEN-2: Records Code
  GEN-3: Teaching Network
  GEN-4: Standards Naming
  GEN-5: Ladder System
TOTAL TITLE CHARS: 1309
TITLE CHECKSUM: 4163231091
DEMO OK

Record demos: each of the 24 records carries its own demo program in the database (Python), with an expected output recorded alongside it; each per-file website re-runs its record's demo.
